{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01AUZvjeE7k2mfHtPQAkKGWC","type":"tool_result","content":"80\t```\n81\t\n82\tThe lead paragraph does not have to sit immediately after the table: a spec\n83\tcarrying a metadata block of its own may place that block between the two. What\n84\tthe convention requires is that prose, rather than a section heading, follows\n85\tthe header material, and that the prose runs to at least 40 characters -\n86\tanything shorter is reported as no lead paragraph at all.\n87\t\n88\t`Issue` names the issue the spec was written against, or `n/a` for the specs\n89\tthat predate this convention.\n90\t\n91\t`Status` is one of `Draft`, `In Progress` or `Shipped`. It is not a mood, and\n92\tit is not a claim about how finished the feature is: it is the spec's own\n93\ttest-citation coverage. No testable statement cited is `Draft`, some cited is\n94\t`In Progress`, all cited is `Shipped`. A spec describing shipped, working\n95\tbehaviour still reads `Draft` until its statements cite the tests that\n96\tvalidate them.\n97\t\n98\tA spec that should no longer be graded takes a terminal value instead. The\n99\tparser buckets roughly sixteen labels into five buckets, and the two terminal\n100\tones skip the coverage check whatever the citation count:\n101\t\n102\t| Written into the spec                                            | Bucket                       |\n103\t| ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- |\n104\t| `Draft`                                                          | `draft`                      |\n105\t| `In Progress`, `In Review`, `Planning`, `WIP`, `Proposed`        | `in-progress`                |\n106\t| `Shipped`, `Implemented`, `Complete`, `Accepted`, `Done`, `Live` | `shipped`                    |\n107\t| `Retired`, `Superseded`, `Removed`, `Deprecated`, `Obsolete`     | `retired` - skips the check  |\n108\t| `Rejected`, `Abandoned`                                          | `rejected` - skips the check |\n109\t\n110\tPrefer the canonical `Draft` / `In Progress` / `Shipped` for a live spec; reach\n111\tfor `Retired` or `Rejected` rather than leaving a superseded spec to claim a\n112\tcoverage tier it will never earn.\n113\t\n114\tADRs do not carry this table. They declare their state as YAML frontmatter\n115\t`status:` instead - `accepted` for a decision the code implements, `proposed`\n116\tfor one it does not. That field does mean maturity, which the spec `Status` row\n117\texplicitly does not. The difference is deliberate: ADRs are exempt from the\n118\tcoverage tier, because asking a decision record to cite a test for every\n119\tstatement it makes is the wrong ask of a decision record.\n120\t\n121\t`.specify/spec.md` is exempt from this section. It is the repo-level system\n122\tspec, it predates the convention, and its content is deliberately frozen -\n123\tgiving it a header table and a lead paragraph would mean writing new prose into\n124\ta file that is meant to stay unchanged. The exemption covers the header table\n125\tand the lead paragraph only: the Lore coverage job does scan\n126\t`.specify/spec.md`, so link placement still applies to it.\n127\t\n128\t## Spec Test Links\n129\t\n130\tStatements in `specs/*/spec.md` cite their validating test in a trailing\n131\tparenthetical at the end of the statement, where a list item counts as one\n132\tstatement and a paragraph counts per sentence. Tests live beside their source\n133\tin this repo, so the path points into `src/`:\n134\t\n135\t```markdown\n136\t- All entries within a single assistant response are committed before the next\n137\t  user message is processed\n138\t  ([validated by: commits every entry before the next user message](../../src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.test.ts#L42)).\n139\t```\n140\t\n141\tThe citation carries the test's **name** as well as its line. The name is what the\n142\tauthor cited; the line is a derived pointer, and it goes stale on its own - adding\n143\tan import above a suite moves every test below it, and the citation lands on a\n144\tdifferent, still-valid declaration without anything noticing. `npm run spec:names`\n145\tholds each citation to the test it names, and `npm run spec:names:fix` repoints the\n146\tline from the name. The `#Lnn` stays because the lint rule and the coverage job\n147\tboth index by it; a citation without one marks the whole file covered rather than\n148\tone test.\n149\t\n150\tA citation counts as coverage only where it is trailing, and a test link placed\n151\tanywhere else in the statement is a misplaced citation rather than a weaker one.\n152\tUpstream `check:spec-links` reports it and exits 1, and the coverage job that\n153\tconsumes these specs records nothing for it - so a spec can read as fully cited while its coverage is\n154\tzero. A mid-statement link to something that is not a test file - a script, a\n155\tdoc, another spec - is ordinary prose, is never reported, and was never coverage\n156\tto begin with. A misplaced citation is fixed by moving it to the end of its\n157\tstatement, never by deleting it.\n158\t\n159\tIn a test file the `#Lnn` anchor must land on the `it(`, `test(` or `describe(`\n160\tdeclaration itself - not a blank line, not one holding only closing punctuation,\n161\tand not a line inside the test body. A citation onto an `expect` renders and\n162\tresolves like any other, and proves nothing: no reader can turn that line back\n163\tinto the name of a case. `scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.mjs` reports all four as\n164\t`rotten` and exits 1 in both modes. A citation into a file that is not a test -\n165\ta workflow, a config, `package.json` - has no declaration to land on and is held\n166\tonly to existing and carrying content.\n167\t\n168\tWhat no gate can catch is an anchor aimed at the wrong declaration: a real but\n169\tunintended `it(` passes every check. The tell is that the test the statement\n170\tmeant to cite goes on reading as uncited in the `require-spec-link` backlog.\n171\t\n172\tAnchors drift silently whenever a cited file gains or loses lines above them, so\n173\tafter editing any cited file re-run `node scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.mjs`\n174\trather than hand-correcting the numbers. That script cannot repair a spec absent\n175\tfrom the base ref, since it has no baseline to translate from; on the branch that\n176\tintroduces a spec, its anchors have to be corrected by hand.\n177\t\n178\tThe convention's tooling is wired up here: `npm run check:spec-links`, `npm run check:spec-status` and\n179\t`node scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.mjs --check origin/main` all gate CI, and"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"21af8b5e-9e5f-422e-9c77-68879100d3de","uuid":"a4b83caf-9fb3-4987-b8b4-cc98ea980973","timestamp":"2026-11-07T17:00:46.660Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/var/tmp/loop-bench/runs/1997d2c32072/workspace/target/AGENTS.md","content":"```\n\nThe lead paragraph does not have to sit immediately after the table: a spec\ncarrying a metadata block of its own may place that block between the two.
